Level trims (dB on `level`) make a full-velocity hit peak near −1 dBFS (hats, shakers and cymbals lower).

Each is checked by render (`drums_presets_sound_like_their_names`, 48 kHz, the dominant frequency 20..130 ms in, the time to fall 40 dB, the spectral centroid of the first 170 ms; `drums_genre_kicks_meet_their_targets`: the crest factor over 300 ms and the pitch drop from the first half cycle to 200..400 ms). Measured 2026-11-11: 808 Kick 47.0 Hz, 1.33 s; 808 Kick Long 47.9 Hz, 2.95 s; 808 Kick Short 52.1 Hz, 0.23 s (the same pitch, a 13× shorter decay); 909 Kick 53.9 Hz, 0.34 s; House Kick 52.1 Hz, 0.34 s; Dubstep Kick 54.0 Hz (the sub inside the 50..60 Hz target), 0.39 s, crest factor 8.3 dB (clipped; the 808 Short is 12.5 dB, the Acoustic Kick 20.3 dB), its first half cycle at 243 Hz falling to 55 Hz by 100 ms; Acoustic Kick 61.3 Hz, 0.19 s; Sub Kick 41.9 Hz; 808 Snare body 180 Hz, centroid 2.8 kHz; 909 Snare 200 Hz, 4.9 kHz; Acoustic Snare 190 Hz head, 1.7 kHz centroid, a full hit at least 15 % brighter than a 0.35 one (`drums_velocity_changes_the_spectrum_and_strike_varies`); Trap Snare 4.0 kHz; Dubstep Snare 185 Hz, 1.05 kHz; 808 Closed Hat 25 ms, 10.7 kHz, Open 190 ms; 909 Closed Hat 13.6 kHz; toms 85, 120, 165 Hz; Crash 1.06 s, 10.9 kHz; Ride 1.38 s; Cowbell 540 Hz, 1.2 kHz; Clap 115 ms, 2.1 kHz; Conga 210 Hz; Bongo 400 Hz; Woodblock 1.1 kHz; Clave 2.5 kHz; Triangle 1.6 kHz, 1.0 s. The reference ranges come from the research below. **[Assumption]** "sounds like its name" is judged by these numbers and the designs' source techniques, not by listening.

### Migration of the old drums

`org.caw.drum.kick`, `.snare`, `.hats` and `.kit` (removed from the registry) migrate in `migrateLegacyDrum` (`builtin_drums_sounds.cpp`), reached through `migrateLegacyInstrument` (project loading, sample packs, `caw_migrate_instrument`; the app's `ProjectDoc.resolveInstruments` lists them in `ComponentUseDoc.retiredInstruments`). The new values reproduce the old Library voices (`caw/library/drums.hpp`, which stay for user code): the kick's sine from `tune` (1 + 10 punch) along the square of a (0.04 + 0.08 punch) s envelope becomes `body_pitch` = 12 log2(1 + 10 punch) st over a pitch time half as long, its noise click a 1.5 kHz Noise click, its drive Soft, its tone a 6 dB low-pass at 150 × 60^tone Hz; the snare's triangle and sine 1.48× above (0.6 : 0.4) a triangle with the second partial at 0.67, and its high-passed noise; the hats' six squares the 808 metal set with `metal_noise` = 1 − metal and the high-pass at q 1.2; the kit's clap a Clap-mode noise (three bursts 10 ms apart, band-pass q 1.5). Old hats by note become a Kit: pad A1 closed on Any other, A2 open on 46, choke group 1; Closed or Open mode become Single. The old kit becomes a Kit with its keys (A1 36 and A2 35 kicks in choke group 2, A3 38 and A5 40 snares in group 3, A4 39 clap, A7 42 and A9 44 closed and A11 46 open hats in group 1), the rest of bank A off, `volume` its volume. Levels and the velocity response carry over (`vel_level` 1, no velocity on tone or click; pan's balance law keeps the centre at full level). Lanes move to `a1_body_freq`, `a1_body_decay`, `a1_click_level`, `a1_drive` (kick), the snare's `a1_body_freq`/`_decay`/`a1_noise_decay`/`_freq`, the hats' `a1_metal_decay` (closed), `a2_metal_decay` (open, by note) and `a1_metal_tone`, and the kit's `a1_`/`a3_`/`a4_`/`a7_`/`a11_` knobs; the old kit's second keys (A2, A5, A9) don't follow a lane. The renders stay close: `the_old_drums_migrate_close_to_how_they_sounded` compares each old drum's reference pattern with its pre-migration measurement (level within 1 dB, bands within 2.5 dB RMS; their noise is drawn from other seeds).

### C API for the drums

All three need no engine; free the result with `caw_free` (signatures in `caw.h`):
- `char* caw_drums_info(void)`: `{"ok", "id", "banks", "pads_per_bank", "pads", "parameters", "pad_parameters", "outputs", "globals": [id], "layers": [{"id", "name", "params": [suffix]}], "types": [name], "sounds": [{"name", "type", "description", "params": {suffix: value, only those not at the empty pad's default}}], "kits": [{"name", "preset", "description", "pads": {pad: sound}}], "samples": [built-in sample names], "gm": [{"note", "name", "pad"}] (keys 35..81), "display": {"version", "floats", "header", "pad_levels", "wave_max", "wave_min", "wave_bins", "pitch", "pitch_points"}}`.
- `char* caw_drums_randomize(int32_t type, uint32_t seed, double amount, int32_t pad, const char* current_json)`: type an index of `types` (−1: the current sound's), pad 0..63, current `{"params": {...}}` (prefixed or bare ids) or NULL; returns `{"ok", "type", "pad", "params": {"<pad>_<suffix>": value}}` with all 162 of the pad's values.
- `char* caw_drums_explode(const char* entry_json)`: see above; `{"ok", "tracks": [{"pad", "name", "notes", "choke", "instrument": {"id", "params", "samples"}}], "kit": {...}}`.

### Display data of the drums

656 floats (`caw_engine_component_display`, about 60 a second, 30 at Eco), layout version 2: 0 layout version (2), 1 mode (0 Kit, 1 Single), 2 the pad shown (0..63: `view_pad`, or the last hit), 3 the glue's gain reduction dB (positive), 4 the shown pad's compressor reduction dB, 5 the waveform's length s, 6 waveform bins (256), 7 pitch points (64), 8 the pitch curve's span s (Curve: three pitch times; Points: 1.15 × the four segments; 0 when the body is off), 9 voices sounding, 10 the last pad hit (−1: none), 11 the last note received (−1: none; any note, a pad's or not: for note learn), 12 its MIDI channel 1..16, 13 its velocity 0..1, 14 a count of notes received (it changes with every note, so the app sees a repeated key), 15 the shown pad's pitch-shape mode (0 Curve, 1 Points); 16..79 each pad's peak since the last publish (0 when silent, so a pad sounding at all reads above 0); 80..335 the shown pad's last hit, each bin's maximum (post-effects with its sub, pre-pan, mono), 336..591 each bin's minimum (the bins fill as the hit plays; their length covers the hit's expected length, 0.05..4 s); 592..655 the shown pad's body pitch in Hz over the span, as a full-velocity hit plays it (Points mode draws the same segments, so the editor's points sit on the curve: point N at the time t1 + .. + tN and the pitch `body_freq` × 2^(pN / 12)).

### Performance

Measured 2026-11-11 with callgrind (instructions in `Engine::process` per second of audio, the Engine and one track, GCC 13 -O2, x86-64; a phone core runs a few billion instructions a second) on a busy 16-pad pattern (24 hits a bar at 120 BPM, every bank-A pad; a sound preset plays every hit on its one pad). This version (deep settings) against the first version on the same bench:

| Load | Eco | Normal | High | First version, Normal |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Kit: 808 | 191 M | 228 M | 318 M | 215 M |
| Kit: Acoustic (resonators, room sends, glue) | | 403 M | | 365 M (its older sounds) |
| Kit: Techno (glue, tape drive, room) | | 429 M | | 396 M |
| Dubstep Kick on all 24 hits (pitch shape, sub, asymmetric drive with filters, clipper) | 131 M | 177 M | 262 M | |
| Acoustic Snare on all 24 hits (mallet membrane, wires, room send) | | 258 M | | |

**1.1.0 (Rook):** `caw_bench SECONDS 323` (and 300; CI `caw_rook_cost`) hits pad A1 every 16th with each new layer, the pad effects and groove: 2.9 to 4.1 ms of CPU per second of audio on the x86 box (2026-11-19; body and noise alone 3.4 ms, so each costs about what an old layer does), ceilings 8 ms. [Assumption] an S22+ core is 2 to 3 times slower: about 1 % of a core per busy pad. RAM: 1.18 MB more per instance at 48 kHz (64 waveguide lines of a 30 Hz period, the echo's 2 s stereo). The FM layer is not oversampled (a deep index aliases at High as at Normal).

The deep settings cost a pad that doesn't use them about 6 % (the voice loop's extra branches and 162 knobs read instead of 119); a plain voice (no sub, width or room send) takes a loop without them. The old org.caw.drum.kit on its own pattern was 45 M; the same project migrated to Drums 69 M. Wall-clock (`caw_bench`-style timing, same machine): Kit: 808 at Normal about 3.7 % of one core. Where it goes: the voices' layers and effects (about 70 %), a pad's knob reading (about 5 %, once a block while knobs hold still). **[Assumption]** on the S22+ about 3 to 5 times the desktop share; not yet measured on the device.

### Research (2026-11-11; sources read on that date)

- **[Fact]** The TR-808 bass drum is a bridged-T network (a band-pass) rung by a 1 ms pulse, with an envelope that moves its centre frequency for the first few milliseconds (Werner, Abel and Smith, "A Physically-Informed, Circuit-Bendable, Digital Model of the Roland TR-808 Bass Drum Circuit", DAFx-14). Here: a swept sine (the body) with a short pitch envelope, rather than a circuit model.
- **[Fact]** The 808's cymbal and hi-hats share a bank of six square oscillators (one hex Schmitt-trigger chip), nominally 205.3, 304.4, 369.6 and 522.7 Hz, two trimmed to 540 and 800 Hz, summed and filtered by band-passes (Werner, Abel and Smith, ICMC-SMC 2014, "The TR-808 cymbal: a physically-informed, circuit-bendable, digital model"). Here: the 808 metal set. **[Assumption, a 2015 Web Audio recreation, outputchannel.com]** the cowbell uses the 540 and 800 Hz pair through a band-pass; the AES 137 paper ("More Cowbell", 2014) was not read in full.
- **[Fact]** Roland describes the TR-909 kick as separate circuits for the attack and the resonant body (an interview quoted by Computer Music); clones list attack, decay, tune decay and level controls (Tiptop BD909). Here: the body's pitch envelope and a separate click layer. **[Assumption]** the 909 kick's wave is a shaped triangle (common knowledge, not checked against a schematic): the 909 Kick preset uses a triangle with `body_shape`. **[Assumption]** the 909's hats were 6-bit samples (widely reported, not checked): the 909 hats are crushed.
- **[Fact]** An ideal circular membrane's modes sit at the zeros of Bessel functions over the lowest (1, 1.593, 2.136, 2.296, 2.653, ...; computed here by bisection, and checked against Sound On Sound's "Synthesizing percussion" and Wikipedia's "Vibration of a circular membrane"); air loading pulls a real drum's toward harmonic. Here: the Membrane model, its strike weights |J_m(j_mn r)|. The plate, bar and tube ratios are textbook (a simply supported rectangular plate, a free-free bar's (β_k / β_1)², a closed pipe's odd harmonics); the bell's are **[Assumption]** typical tuned-bell partials; the cymbal model's spread is made up.
- **[Fact, reviews and tutorials, MusicRadar and Audiotent]** Sonic Academy Kick 2: a sub with a pitch envelope drawn as a Bezier curve with snap-to-note, up to three click layers, key tracking to tune the kick to the song's key. Here: `body_pcurve`, the click layer, `track` with `root`. **[Fact, Microtonic 3.1 manual, manualsdir.com]** Microtonic: an oscillator and a noise section with a multimode filter and three envelope modes, an oscillator/noise mix and velocity to the noise. Here: the noise layer's modes and filters. Arturia DrumBrute/Spark, Ableton Drum Sampler/DS Kick, D16 Drumazon/Nepheton, Elektron's machines, AudioRealism ADM and Bazzism were not researched in detail this session: **[Assumption]** their common controls (tune, decay, pitch envelope amount and time, click, drive, tone, choke and open/closed hats) are covered above.
- **[Fact]** General MIDI's percussion map (keys 35..81 on channel 10) and the default pad layout: see the note map above.
- **Per type, what the controls are for (read 2026-11-11):**
  - **Kicks, layered.** **[Fact, production guides: soundbridge.io "How to layer kick drums", Ableton's "Make a perfect kick drum" (Lenny Kiser)]** a modern kick is built from a top layer (the attack: click and punch), a body layer (tone, around 130..170 Hz of "punch") and a sub layer (the low end), with a slight fade at the start of the sub so it doesn't fight the attack, and their phase kept aligned. **[Fact, Cymatics "How to make dubstep"]** a dubstep kick must cut through the bass: its weight sits at about 50..60 Hz. **[Assumption, common practice not checked against one source]** riddim and dubstep kicks are clipped hard (a low crest factor) with the distortion kept off the sub. Here: the body as the punch layer with a drawn pitch shape, the Clean sub with its own attack (5 ms default), the drive's low cut (the sub stays clean) and mix, and the clipper; the Dubstep Kick preset measures a 54 Hz fundamental and an 8.3 dB crest factor.
  - **808 long and short.** **[Fact, Werner, Abel and Smith, DAFx-14, above]** the 808 kick is one decaying swept tone whose decay knob sets its length; **[Assumption]** trap and drill use it decays of 2..4 s, electro and house under 0.4 s. Here: the same body, the decay apart (2.95 s against 0.23 s).
  - **Acoustic kick and snare.** **[Fact, Fletcher and Bassett's bass-drum study (JASA; physics.byu.edu abstract) and Rossing, "The acoustics of percussion instruments", Physics Today 45(3), 1992]** a bass drum is two membranes and the air between them, its spectrum changing with where and how hard it is struck; a snare's lowest modes are pairs of the (0,1) and (1,1) membrane modes coupled through the air, and the snare wires buzz against the lower head. **[Fact, mixing guides, gear4music "Kick drum EQ" and soundsheavy "Drum EQ frequencies"]** a kick's weight sits at 50..100 Hz, its boxiness at 200..400 Hz and the beater's click at 2..5 kHz (felt lower than plastic); a snare's body at 150..250 Hz. Here: the Membrane resonator (struck off centre, its position varied per hit), the head's short pitch drop, the Pop click, the boxiness cut at 400 Hz, Snappy wires that follow the head, velocity on brightness and decay, and the room send. **[Assumption]** a stick's contact lasts a few milliseconds (a thesis on snare drums gives 3..6 ms; KTH master's thesis, Wagner 2006): the mallet exciter lasts 0.5..2.5 ms, longer for a soft hit.
  - **Cymbals.** **[Fact, Sound On Sound "Analysing metallic percussion"; Rossing and coauthors on gongs and cymbals (Legge and Fletcher 1989, UNSW)]** cymbal modes are inharmonic; a hard hit spreads energy upward into a near-noise spectrum over the first few hundred milliseconds, the high modes die first and the last energy sits in the few-hundred-hertz modes; hi-hats are clamped, rides pivot and crashes swing freely. Here: FM-paired or square metal plus noise, the Cymbal resonator, decay curves for the fast-then-long fall, and pressure to damp or choke. **[Assumption]** the upward cascade itself (a slow build of high partials) isn't modelled; the noise share stands in for it.
- **Not found:** a measured beater contact time for bass drums, and published decay times for crash and ride; both are **[Assumption]** values tuned by measurement against the ranges above.

## Audio assets, clips, the sampler and audio input (API 18)

Roadmap 0.2, 3.1–3.5, 4.2–4.3 and 7.2–7.7 on the engine side (WP-J). Code: `asset_store.*`, `time_stretch.*`, `clip_stretch.*` (Signalsmith Stretch), `audio_analysis.*` (tempo, slices and the key), `sample_preview.*` (the sample browser's preview), `audio_clips.*`, `builtin_sampler.cpp`, `builtin_sampler_sfz.cpp`, `audio_input.*`, `capi_audio.cpp`, `utf8_path.h` (UTF-8 paths and 64-bit file positions), `platform/decode_miniaudio.c` the separate capture device and its input bridge (`input_bridge.h`) in `platform/audio_miniaudio.cpp`. Tests: `engine/tests/audio_assets_test.cpp`, `audio_stretch_test.cpp` (API 32: the bypass, stretch and pitch, chaotic references `references/audio_stretch.cawref` for a drum and a tonal loop, a ramp, edits, detection, slices, parsing limits, stretchers published while playing), `input_bridge_test.cpp` (the output unchanged when the input dies, reopen, no NaN or clicks, drift) `sample_library_test.cpp` (API 33: the key estimate, the preview's options, playback, seek, stop, stretch, beat and bar sync and the route through a track) and `audio_rt_test.cpp` and `sample_preview_rt_test.cpp` (none of it allocates on the audio thread).

### Audio assets

- **Decoding:** WAV (PCM 8–32-bit, float, extensible), FLAC and MP3 through miniaudio 0.11.25's bundled dr_wav, dr_flac and dr_mp3 ([decisions](decisions.md#2026-11-11-audio-assets-clips-sampler-and-input-wp-j-choices-to-confirm)), compiled a second time inside the core with every function `static`, so it never meets the device copy. Files are untrusted: at most 1 GB on disk and 3 hours long, every read bounds-checked by the decoders, samples clamped to ±8 and non-finite ones made 0; a file that fails gives an error, never a crash. The decoder sets its limits from the file's real rate and channel count once it has read the header, keeps only the first two channels while decoding, and stops as soon as the kept samples (4 bytes × at most two channels) would pass what the store's budget has left (the budget less the assets in use) or 3 hours; the resampled planes are checked against the same before they are made (a fuzz test feeds random bytes behind a RIFF header).
- **Resampling:** once, at load, to the engine's rate: a Kaiser-windowed sinc (β 9, 32 zero crossings at the lower rate, cutoff 0.96 of its Nyquist), deterministic maths (`detSin`). A source at 4× the engine rate or more is first halved by 31-tap Kaiser halfband stages (β 11, passband to 0.12 and stopband from 0.38 of the stage's rate) until it is below 4×, so the final kernel stays at most about 133 taps a side (768 kHz to 48 kHz directly was about 1,070). More than two channels keep the first two; mono stays mono (heard in both sides).
- **The store** (`AssetStore::global()`, one per process): an asset is keyed by path and engine rate and reloaded when the file's size or time changes; clips and zones naming the same file share one copy (refcounted). The budget (1 GiB by default, `caw_asset_store_set_budget`) counts decoded bytes; when a load needs room the least recently used asset nothing plays is dropped, and a load that still doesn't fit is refused with a message. Decoding happens on whatever thread asks (the app's worker isolate for `caw_asset_load`, or the control thread during a project update): preload long files with `caw_asset_load` so the update finds them cached.
- **Peaks:** min/max per 256 frames per channel, then levels of 512, 1,024, … frames; `caw_asset_peaks` answers any range and bucket count from the coarsest level no wider than a bucket (or from the samples when zoomed further in).

### Audio clips

A layer with `"audio_clips"` (and/or `"input"`) is an audio track: the engine plays it with the built-in instrument `org.caw.audio-track` (internal: `caw_engine_components` never lists it; it can't also name an `instrument`; a folder can't have clips), so a clip goes through the track's inserts, volume, pan, sends, folder buses, sidechain keys and latency compensation like any instrument, live and in every render (song renders, stems, exports).

```json
"assets": [ { "id": "a1", "path": "/abs/loop.wav", "bpm": 120 } ],
"layers": [ { "id": "t1", "name": "Vox", "volume": 0.8,
  "input": { "channel": 0, "channels": 1, "monitor": "auto", "armed": true },
  "audio_clips": [ { "id": "c1", "asset": "a1", "start_steps": 16, "length_steps": 32, "offset_seconds": 0.25,
    "gain": 1, "fade_in_seconds": 0.01, "fade_in_curve": "linear", "fade_out_seconds": 0.5, "fade_out_curve": "equal_power",
    "reverse": false, "loop": false, "loop_start_seconds": 0, "loop_end_seconds": 0, "pitch": 0,
    "mode": "fixed", "stretch": "wsola", "source_bpm": 120, "mute": false,
    "follows_tempo": false, "stretch_ratio": 1, "pitch_cents": 0, "gain_db": 0,
    "loop_start_frames": 4410, "loop_end_frames": 0 } ] } ]
```

- `"assets"` (project level, optional) names files once: `id`, absolute `path`, optional `bpm` (the source tempo musical clips default to). A clip names `"asset"` or a `"path"` directly. An unknown asset id refuses the project; a file that fails to load leaves its clip silent (the rest plays).
- **Placement:** `start_steps` and `length_steps` are sixteenths and may be fractional (a recorded take lands off the grid); the sample is `floor(steps × samples per step + 0.5)`, as notes. The song lengthens to reach the last clip's end, except a clip with `"lengthens_song": false` (1.0.5: a frozen track's, whose tail past the song's end plays when the song reaches there or in a render's tail). At most 4,096 clips a track and 16 sounding at once (more overlapping are skipped).
- **Source:** `offset_seconds` into the file at its own speed; `reverse` reads the file backwards (offset and loop points then count from its end); `loop` repeats `[loop_start_seconds, loop_end_seconds)` (0 end: the file's end) to fill the clip, or `[loop_start_frames, loop_end_frames)` in frames of the file at its own rate when given (API 32; whole numbers 0..2^40, end 0 the file's end; they replace the seconds); past the file's end without a loop is silence.
- **Level:** `gain` linear 0..3.98 (+12 dB), times `gain_db` (−96..36 dB, API 32: Normalise sets it); fades in seconds of output with curves `linear`, `equal_power` (sine), `s_curve` (raised cosine), `fast` (1 − (1 − u)²), `slow` (u²).
- **Speed and pitch:** `mode` `"fixed"` plays at the file's speed divided by `stretch_ratio` (0.25..4, API 32: output length over source length; a tempo change moves where it starts, never how fast it plays, and a fixed clip already sounding carries on from the source position it is at; the app sets `length_steps`); `"musical"`, or `"follows_tempo": true` (API 32; with `"mode": "fixed"` it is refused), follows the tempo and ignores `stretch_ratio`: with one tempo the source moves `bpm / source_bpm` times real time; under a tempo map of more than one tempo the source position at song step s is `offset + (s − start_steps) × 15 / source_bpm` seconds, so the clip stays on the bars through steps and ramps, its rate (the tempo there / `source_bpm`) worked out again every 64-frame chunk (needs `source_bpm` or the asset's `bpm`). `pitch` (semitones −48..48) plus `pitch_cents` (−2400..2400, API 32; the sum within ±48) transposes without changing length. `stretch` names the algorithm: `"wsola"` (the default), `"signalsmith"` (API 32: Signalsmith Stretch, below) or `"varispeed"`, which resamples, so pitch moves with speed (a musical varispeed clip at another tempo is transposed by it; in a fixed clip the pitch changes the speed too, and the app should change its length). A clip at its file's speed with no pitch copies samples exactly whatever `stretch` says (the bypass; `stretch_ratio_1_with_no_pitch_plays_the_samples_exactly_whatever_the_algorithm`); one following a tempo map never takes that path, so its reader doesn't change on a ramp.
- **The readers** (`time_stretch.*`): exact copies; cubic Hermite (Catmull-Rom) for varispeed, with no anti-alias filter when reading faster than real time; WSOLA (Verhelst and Roelands 1993, as reviewed by Driedger and Müller 2016): 30 ms periodic-Hann frames at 50% overlap whose analysis positions follow the arrangement, each moved by up to half a hop to best continue the last (cross-correlation of the mono sum, every 4th lag on every 4th sample, then refined to the sample), then a Hermite pitch stage; Signalsmith Stretch (below). Direct and varispeed reading hold no state, so playback from anywhere sounds as passing through; a WSOLA voice starts afresh at a seek or a loop wrap (deterministic from that point). Everything is the same at every block size (tested at 37, 64 and 512; Signalsmith voices too, for a clip that follows no tempo map). WSOLA at half speed can drop or move a 2 ms click by up to a hop (seen in the ramp test, which therefore checks varispeed and Signalsmith).
- **Signalsmith Stretch** (API 32, `src/clip_stretch.*`; the library is vendored with two marked local changes, [decisions](decisions.md)): its default preset (120 ms blocks every 30 ms, Kaiser windows), stereo, with the computation split across each interval, and pitch with a tonality limit of 8 kHz. It and the clip readers (`time_stretch.cpp`) are built with the strict float flags, and its complex exponentials (FFT twiddles, per-block rotations) come from the engine's deterministic sine instead of libm, so a stretched clip gives the same bits on Linux x64, Linux Arm64 and Windows. Its random phases (past a 2x stretch) come from `std::minstd_rand`, reseeded at every start, through a fixed mapping to floats (not `std::uniform_real_distribution`, whose algorithm each standard library picks), so a replay gives the same bits and every platform the same draws (`signalsmith_replays_bit_identical_past_a_2x_stretch` pins them). It runs **live in the audio callback**, its `process()` measured to allocate nothing (`signalsmith_clip_voices_never_allocate_on_the_audio_thread`). **Stretchers** (about 0.9 MB each at 48 kHz) belong to the track's timeline: a project update sizes the pool to the most Signalsmith clips that overlap (each with a step of pre-roll, at most the cap) plus two (a voice fading out, a spare for the loop's start), keeps the last timeline's stretchers that are at the engine's rate and prepares the rest on the control thread; those it drops are freed with the old timeline, on the control thread (`a_smaller_stretcher_pool_is_freed_off_the_audio_thread`). **At most 8 Signalsmith voices a track** play at once (**[Assumption]** enough for a track's overlapping loops; a voice costs about 1.1% of this container's core, and the S22+'s figure is still to measure); more play WSOLA, and a voice on WSOLA takes a stretcher as soon as one is free, bridged as below (`a_track_plays_at_most_eight_signalsmith_voices_and_upgrades_when_one_frees`). **Feeding:** a voice feeds the stretcher the source ahead of the output (by output frame t, the source up to s(t + 4320) + 2880 at 48 kHz: its output and input latencies), so its output at t is the source at s(t); each 64-frame chunk (aligned to the voice's start, kept in a 2048-frame ring) is fed from the mapping at that chunk's own frame, through the tempo map itself for a clip that follows one, so a ramp renders the same at any block size (`a_clip_following_a_ramp_renders_the_same_at_every_block_size`); source frames before the clip's offset read as silence. **Starting:** its output is good 4320 frames (90 ms) after a start. A voice for a clip ahead of the playhead starts that early, silent (the pre-roll), and so does a spare for each clip sounding where the loop lands, a pre-roll before the wrap (in the time it will have after it), which takes over at the wrap: a loop's second pass plays the same bits as the clip reached from ahead (`a_loop_wrap_starts_signalsmith_from_a_preroll_not_a_bridge`). A voice that starts mid-clip (play from inside it, a seek, a pitch change) plays WSOLA meanwhile (the bridge); at 4320 + 256 frames Signalsmith's output is moved by up to ±256 frames to the lag that best matches WSOLA's next 256 frames, the two crossfade over 512 frames with gains that keep the sum's power for their measured correlation (linear gains a, b scaled by 1 / sqrt(a² + b² + 2ρab)), and the lag is then taken back out of the feed over 0.5 s (a rate change of at most 1%), so the voice ends exactly on its mapping. Through the crossfade a steady sine stays within 1 dB of Signalsmith alone in every 128 frames, and a chord within 1.5 dB in 2048-frame windows (no single lag lines up a phase vocoder's partials with WSOLA's; the linear crossfade before dipped 5 to 8 dB; `the_bridge_into_signalsmith_keeps_its_level_through_the_crossfade`). Measured: clicks land within 2.5 ms of where the map puts them through a 120 → 60 BPM ramp (varispeed within 0.3 ms); pitch shifts and ratios hold the frequency within 1%. **[Fact, Signalsmith's README, read 2026-11-14]** time-stretching sounds best between 0.75x and 1.5x.
- **Declick:** a clip voice cut by a seek, a loop wrap, stop, a mode change, a mute or an edit fades out over 4 ms, reading on where it was (the player keeps its own continuous time), while a voice that starts mid-clip fades in over 4 ms; a voice starting at its clip's start is left as authored. A clip's `loop` seam crossfades over 4 ms (at most half the loop) and keeps the loop's period: into the audio before `loop_start` when there is some, else from the audio after `loop_end`, else (the whole file looped) a dip of 2 ms out and 2 ms in. On a tempo change, musical clips follow the new grid, declicked. The 4 ms is to confirm ([decisions](decisions.md#2026-11-11-audio-review-fixes-declick-lengths-to-confirm)).
- **Edits** from roadmap 3.4 (trim, split, move, duplicate, offset, loop, fade, gain, reverse) are these fields: the original file is never changed (3.5). Selection, snapping, grouping and undo are the app's.

### Tempo detection and slices

API 32 (`src/audio_analysis.*`, `caw_asset_tempo`, `caw_asset_slices`): offline analysis of a loaded asset for the app's clip tempo and Slice to MIDI, on the caller's thread (a worker isolate). Options come as JSON (NULL for the defaults; at most 4096 bytes, an object, each value in range or the call fails naming it); at most 600 s are read.

- **Tempo** (`min_bpm` 30..300, default 70; `max_bpm` default 180, above `min_bpm` and at most 4 times it; `start_seconds`, `end_seconds`): a spectral-flux novelty curve (log-compressed magnitudes of 1024-point Hann frames every 256 samples at 48 kHz; silence before the file's start, so a hit at the start counts), its slow trend (0.25 s) removed and half-wave rectified, then its autocorrelation with the mean removed; each tempo from min to max in 0.1 BPM steps scores the mean of the autocorrelation at its first eight beat periods, less half the autocorrelation at half a period when twice the tempo is in range (so a click at 174 isn't read as 87), times a gentle preference for tempos near 120 between octaves; the best three peaks refined on a 0.01 BPM grid, rounded to 0.01 and to a whole BPM when within 0.1 of one. `bpm` is null (unsure) with fewer than 4 onsets or a confidence (the best score without the preference) under 0.2. Tested on click tracks at 90, 97.5, 120, 128, 140 and 174 BPM (within 0.5) and a drum loop at 120; noise and silence come back unsure.
- **Slices** (`sensitivity` 0..1, default 0.5; `start_seconds`, `end_seconds`; `min_gap_seconds` 0.005..2, default 0.05; `max_slices` 1..256, default 64): a finer flux curve (512-point frames every 128 samples), started on the audio before the range where there is some (a sound already playing at the range's start is no onset); a peak counts above the moving median (0.2 s) plus a threshold of 0.02 + 0.98 (1 − sensitivity)² of the curve's peak, the stronger of two closer than the gap wins, the strongest `max_slices` are kept in time order (one within 5 ms of the range's start is left out: that start is the first cut), and each moves back to the start of its attack (the loudest sample near it, then back while the signal stays above a tenth of it). Each slice has `seconds` from the file's start, `frame` at the file's own rate and `strength` 0..1. Tested on decaying noise hits at known times (within 2 ms; a hit 35 dB down found only at a high sensitivity). Both read the asset where it lies in one streaming pass (no copy of the range): 600 s at 48 kHz took 2.7 MB at most for tempo and 1.9 MB for slices beside the asset's own 230 MB, 5.8 and 5.4 s (this container, Release, 2026-11-14).

### The sample preview and key estimate

API 33 (#229; batch 1.0.5; the buffer tuner, #236, follows as 34) for the app's sample browser ([app](app.md)).

- **Key estimate** (`caw_asset_key`, `detectKey` in `src/audio_analysis.*`; options `start_seconds`, `end_seconds`, limits as above, at most 600 s read): the mono sum box-filtered and decimated to a quarter of the rate, 8192-point Hann frames every 4096 samples; each bin from 55 Hz to 2 kHz adds its log-compressed magnitude (log(1 + 1000 m)) to its nearest pitch class, weighted cos² by its distance from the semitone; the summed chromagram (its largest 1) is correlated (Pearson) with the Krumhansl–Kessler major and minor profiles (C. L. Krumhansl, *Cognitive Foundations of Musical Pitch*, 1990) rotated to each of the 12 tonics. The answer is an estimate, never metadata: `confidence` is the winner's lead over the runner-up (×5, at most 1) times its correlation; `sure` needs a correlation of at least 0.5, a lead of 0.03 and some energy, so silence, a short blip and noise come back unsure (the app then shows no key). The five best candidates come back too. Tested on chord progressions in C, G, A minor and F♯ minor and on an A minor triad through the C API.
- **Preview voice** (`src/sample_preview.*`, owned by the Engine; `caw_engine_preview_sample`, `_stop`, `_seek`, `_volume`, `_state`): one file at a time beside the song, playing or stopped, as the test tone. A start loads the file through the asset store and, for a `stretch_ratio` other than 1 (0.25..4; a file over 60 s plays at its own speed), renders it once with the clips' Signalsmith reader on the calling thread (a worker isolate), sanitised, so a preview sounds as the clip will. Starts, stops and seeks go through a 64-command lock-free queue; the audio thread takes them once per callback and plays a current voice and a fading one, so a start, stop or seek crossfades over 256 samples; the volume (0.8 at first) is smoothed. `sync` `"beat"` or `"bar"` starts it on the song's next beat or bar while the song plays (past a count-in; from the tempo map and the signature at the playhead; a loop wrap before that beat is not looked at), at once while stopped. Each start is numbered (the answer's `seq`; `_state` reads the number of the start playing), so a caller knows when its own start has ended. Prepared sounds are freed on a control thread once the audio thread has moved past them. Mixed after the master's gain and the transport's fades and before the safety limiter (`runMasterNode`, or `mixToneOnly` when the master stage doesn't run).
- **Through a track** (option `"layer"`, the browser's FX toggle): the voice is added to that track's instrument output in its graph node, so its inserts, volume and pan shape it; stopped, the track is woken as a monitoring track is and runs until its tail is quiet. A muted or soloed-out track doesn't run, and the preview then joins the master as without it; an unknown id plays straight (`"layer": false` in the answer). `_state`'s `through` says whether the last callback went through a track. The slot is looked up when the preview starts: a project change that moves the track needs a new start (the app starts one per tap).
- **Tests:** `sample_library_test.cpp` (the four progressions, silence and a blip unsure, option limits, playback at the preview volume, seek, volume, stop's fade, a one-shot ending and a loop going on, a 2x stretch, a start on the next beat at 120 BPM exactly and a wait for the bar, the route through a hard-left half-volume track stopped and playing, muted and unknown tracks), `sample_preview_rt_test.cpp` (starts, seeks, stops, volume, stretch, sync and the route never allocate on the audio thread).

### Sampler (`org.caw.sampler`)

An instrument (one note input, stereo out) that plays zones over the asset store. Its state (`"state"` base64 in the instrument entry, `caw_engine_set_component_state` while it plays, version 1) is JSON `{"zones": [zone, ...]}` (at most 4,096 zones, 4 MB). A project update with a changed state reloads the zones of a kept sampler; voices of the old zones fade out over 5 ms on them (the sampler keeps the old zones until they are done).

Zone keys mirror SFZ opcodes; positions are frames of the sample file at its own rate:

| Key | Meaning (default) |
|---|---|
| `path` | the sample file (required) |
| `lokey`, `hikey`, `pitch_keycenter` | key range 0..127 (0, 127) and root (60) |
| `lovel`, `hivel` | velocity range 1..127 |
| `tune`, `transpose`, `pitch_keytrack` | cents, semitones, cents per key (100) |
| `volume`, `pan`, `amp_veltrack` | dB, −100..100 (balance), velocity sensitivity in % (100) |
| `offset`, `end` | first and last frame played (end 0: the file's end) |
| `loop_mode` | `no_loop`, `one_shot` (note-off ignored), `loop_continuous`, `loop_sustain` (loops while held, then plays on); loop points without a mode loop continuously |
| `loop_start`, `loop_end`, `loop_crossfade` | the loop's first and last frame; crossfade seconds (equal power, into the audio before the loop start) |
| `seq_length`, `seq_position` | round robins: a zone plays on hit `seq_position` of every `seq_length` matching ones (each zone counts its own hits) |
| `lorand`, `hirand` | random round robin: plays when a per-note random 0..1 (seeded, deterministic) falls in [lorand, hirand) |
| `group`, `off_by` | choke: a zone of group g cuts voices whose zone has off_by g (5 ms) |
| `ampeg_attack`, `ampeg_decay`, `ampeg_sustain` (%), `ampeg_release` | this zone's envelope instead of the instrument's |
| `stretch`, `stretch_bpm` | C.A.W's own (API 43; not written to SFZ): `stretch` true keeps the zone's length on every key, the key moving only its pitch; `stretch_bpm` (0, or the sample's tempo 20..999) also plays it at the song's tempo over that one |

Parameters: `gain` (dB −60..12), `attack`, `decay`, `sustain` (0..1), `release`, `velocity` (sensitivity 0..1), `tune` (cents ±100), `transpose` (±48), `filter` (Off, Low-pass, High-pass, Band-pass: the Library's SVF per voice), `cutoff` (20..20,000 Hz), `resonance` (Q 0.5..20), `filter_env` (octaves ±8), `filter_key` (key tracking 0..1), `filter_attack`/`decay`/`sustain`/`release`, `polyphony` (1..64; at the limit the oldest releasing voice is stolen, else the oldest, and it fades out over 5 ms in its slot while the new note starts in one of 8 spare slots; voices fading out do not count). A note that matches several zones layers them. Samples are read with cubic Hermite interpolation. Presets (1.1.0, knobs only, no zones): One-shot drums, Soft pad, Plucky keys, Mono sample bass, Vocal chops, Guitar strum, Dusty sampler, Big bird.

**Stretched zones** (`builtin_sampler.cpp`): a voice on a `stretch` zone reads through the clips' `ClipReader` (`time_stretch.h`) with its pitch (key, tune, transpose and the parameters, fixed at the note's start: a pitch bend does not move it) and the zone's speed (1, or the song's tempo over `stretch_bpm`, followed block by block), then the same envelopes and filter. A sampler whose zones stretch keeps, built once off the audio thread when the first such program loads: 16 readers (about 110 KB each at 48 kHz) and 4 Signalsmith Stretch instances. A note takes a free reader (none: it plays as a plain voice) and a free stretcher (none: WSOLA, `ReadMode::Stretch`); a Signalsmith voice starts on WSOLA and crosses to Signalsmith once its latency has passed, as a clip joining mid-way does. Loops play continuously (`loop_sustain` too). Cost (`caw_bench SECONDS 308`, the whole engine with four held notes, calibration-box µs per second of audio, 2026-11-19): plain zone about 5,900; four Signalsmith voices about 72,000 (about 17,000 a voice, heavy; WSOLA about 3,500 a voice). Tests: `engine/tests/sampler_tools_test.cpp` (length kept on every key, the tempo, 1/37/64/512-frame blocks within 1e-4), `audio_rt_test.cpp` (no allocation with more stretched notes than stretchers).

### Sampler tools (API 43)

Offline helpers for the sampler's editor (`sampler_tools.*`, any thread but the audio thread; each loads its files as `caw_asset_load` does and answers the sampler's state JSON):

- **Root detection** (`detectRoot`): YIN (de Cheveigné and Kawahara, JASA 2002) on the mono sum, 50 ms frames every 25 ms from 30 ms after the loudest attack, up to 1 s; the median pitch of the frames with aperiodicity under 0.2 and within 40 dB of the loudest. Sure when at least 3 frames, and half of those looked at, agree within half a semitone; the zone is then tuned by the cents off its rounded note. Tested from E2 to C7, 30 cents sharp, 44.1 kHz, noise and silence.
- **Drop and play** (`caw_sampler_map`): one sample covers keys 0..127 at its root (C4 when none is found); several are split halfway between neighbouring roots (the ends reaching the keyboard's ends; samples sharing a root become round robins) when every root is known, else spread over equal key ranges, each rooted at its range's middle. Modes `root`, `spread` and `keys` (one key each from `start_key`) force a choice; a given `root` skips detection.
- **Slicing** (`caw_sampler_slice`): cuts by transients (`detectSlices`, the Slice to MIDI detector) or an equal grid; slice n plays one shot on key `start_key` + n at its own pitch; the answer's slices (file frames) let the app put them on Drums pads.
- **Recorded takes** (`caw_sampler_take`): for each planned note, its window of the take is searched for the note (its attack: the first frame within 26 dB of the window's peak, less 2 ms; its end: the last frame within 54 dB, plus 20 ms; quieter than −60 dBFS is missing), its pitch checked (`in_tune`: within half a semitone of the key asked for), and the zones made by root per velocity layer.

Requests are at most 64 KiB (128 samples or notes); an out-of-range value fails with an error naming its key.

### SFZ support

`caw_sfz_import` reads a `.sfz` (at most 4 MB) into the state above: `<control>` (`default_path`), `<global>`, `<master>`, `<group>`, `<region>` with inheritance, `//` and `/* */` comments, `#define $NAME` (expanded in one pass: at each `$` the longest defined name, and a replacement is not expanded again; refused past 16 MB expanded), note names (`c4` = 60, `#` and `b`), sample paths with spaces and backslashes, relative to the file's folder. Opcodes read: `sample`, `key`, `lokey`, `hikey`, `pitch_keycenter`, `lovel`, `hivel`, `tune`, `transpose`, `volume`, `pan`, `pitch_keytrack`, `amp_veltrack`, `offset`, `end`, `loop_mode`/`loopmode`, `loop_start`/`loopstart`, `loop_end`/`loopend`, `loop_crossfade`, `seq_length`, `seq_position`, `lorand`, `hirand`, `group`, `off_by`, `ampeg_attack`, `ampeg_decay`, `ampeg_sustain`, `ampeg_release`, `trigger=attack`. Left out, each with a warning: regions with another `trigger` (release, first, legato), `#include`, `<curve>`/`<effect>`/`<midi>`/`<sample>` sections and every other opcode (named with its count: filters, LFOs, per-region EGs beyond ampeg, CC modulation, crossfades by key or velocity). Missing samples are warned about too. `caw_sfz_export` writes the same opcodes back (paths relative to the `.sfz` where inside its folder; `copy_samples` copies the files, unchanged, into `samples/`, named uniquely against what is already there without regard to case and never over an existing file, and only regular `.wav`/`.flac`/`.mp3` files whose first bytes say so, up to 1 GB, since a project's zone path could otherwise copy any readable file into a shared folder); frame opcodes are clamped to 9e15 (as are `caw_asset_peaks` times) before the integer cast; export then import gives the same state (tested).

### Audio input and recording

- **The device** (owner, 2026-11-12, [decisions](decisions.md#2026-11-12-audio-keeps-playing-when-the-microphone-is-busy-or-the-app-is-behind-owner)): the input is a capture device of its own beside the playback device (never miniaudio duplex), opened and closed alone by `caw_audio_configure_input` while the output plays on: any capture device the backend lists (AAudio: the default input, which Android routes), 1..32 channels, shared mode, the engine's rate. Its callback pushes into the input bridge (`engine/src/input_bridge.h`, a lock-free single-producer, single-consumer ring of 65,536 samples); the output callback pulls the input for each block it renders (`DeviceRender::render`), the drift loop sampling the fill once a callback, at its first pull (`InputBridge::beginCallback`; sampled at every pull the fill read about half a callback low, `input_bridge_samples_its_fill_once_per_output_callback`), and whatever is missing (closed, starting, starved, silenced, disconnected) is silence, so the output's sound never depends on the input (`input_disconnect_while_playing_leaves_the_output_unchanged`, bit for bit). The output starts reading once two capture periods plus one output callback (the larger of the backend's period and miniaudio's fixed callback size), plus what the output's block FIFO renders ahead (up to 63 frames), are queued (in the engine's frames: the bridge runs at the engine's rate). The two devices' clocks drift by up to a few hundred ppm, so the output reads at a slightly changing rate (4-point Hermite interpolation), steered by a slow PI loop on the smoothed fill (poles near 0.3 rad/s, at most 2000 ppm), which holds the fill, and the input's delay, at that starting fill with no gaps, splices or creep (`input_bridge_follows_clock_drift_with_no_gaps_splices_or_creep`: ±100 and ±500 ppm for five minutes, the delay within one capture period). Only a real discontinuity is faded: a start or a refill after the input ran dry fades in and out over 64 frames, and a queue eight periods past the fill (the output stalled while capture ran on) jumps back through a 64-frame crossfade. NaN/Inf become 0. Device opens and closes run under an operation lock, the shared state under the device lock only for the swap, so `caw_audio_status` and `caw_audio_latency` never wait for a microphone to open; before any device is uninitialised, reroute jobs miniaudio queued for it are drained ([Fact] miniaudio 0.11.25 source, read 2026-11-12: a reroute job posted from the AAudio error callback could otherwise run after `ma_device_uninit` destroyed the device's reroute mutex). `missing_frames` counts output frames the input was missing since it first delivered, so a take's gaps can be told.
- **Behind the app (API 26):** `caw_audio_set_background(e, 1)` raises the AAudio output's buffer size to the stream's capacity with `AAudioStream_setBufferSizeInFrames` (no reopen; [Fact] developer.android.com NDK AAudio reference, read 2026-11-12: it adjusts the latency up to `getBufferCapacityInFrames`, API 26), and 0 restores the size it had; it returns the size now (0 off AAudio). The app calls it while playing behind the app with no take running (nothing monitors there) and refreshes the latency compensation either way. A reroute meanwhile opens a stream at its default size. If the input won't open (no permission, no microphone, another app holding it) the output plays on and `caw_audio_status`'s `"input"` says why. Its `"state"`: `off`, `open`, `stalled` (open, nothing arrived for 250 ms), `stopped` (the system stopped its device) or `failed`; with `underruns` and `dropped_frames`. Called again with the same arguments while the input is not `open`, it reopens the input alone. **Android needs the RECORD_AUDIO permission first** (the app asks).
- **Why not duplex** ([Fact], miniaudio 0.11.25 source, read 2026-11-12): in duplex mode on AAudio the playback side calls the data callback only with frames the capture side has delivered (`ma_device__handle_duplex_callback_playback`), so a late or silenced microphone made the output drop out, more the longer it played; and a reroute (`ma_device_reinit__aaudio`) closes both streams and, after failing to reopen, stops the device. `caw_audio_status`'s `"running"` now comes from `ma_device_get_state` (with `"stopped_by_system"`), and `caw_audio_start` reopens a device the system stopped.
- **A track's `"input"`:** `channel` (first device channel, from 0), `channels` 1 (heard in both sides) or 2, `monitor` `off`, `on` (always) or `auto` (while armed and stopped, or armed while recording), `armed`. Monitoring plays the input through the track's whole chain in the output callback that pulls it from the bridge (the bridge's starting fill added to the devices' own buffers); while stopped a monitoring track keeps running as a held preview does. Armed with `monitor` not `off`, its effects play their near-zero-delay versions (API 44, "Around the effects"). Input meters: `caw_engine_input_peaks`.
- **Recording** (`caw_record_start` with the armed tracks): each chunk the transport plays, the audio thread copies the input into a lock-free ring (2 s of the channels used) with its song position, and a writer thread writes each track's take as WAV (32-bit float, or 24-bit PCM), `<layer>-take<n>.wav.part`, renamed to `.wav` when the take ends, with a `.wav.json` sidecar (layer, take, start). A full ring drops frames (counted in `dropped_frames`) and the writer fills them with silence at the song positions they had, carried by a sample-less header, so a take never slips or splits at a loop wrap or punch while the disk lags (`a_full_ring_across_loop_wraps_keeps_each_take_whole_and_in_place`; only when the 8,192-entry header queue is full too are positions assumed to run on); a failed write (full storage) ends that take with an error and keeps what was written. File positions are 64-bit, and a take goes on in a new file (the next take number, starting where the last ended) before its WAV sizes would pass 4 GB (`max_file_bytes` in the request lowers that limit, at least 1024). Paths are UTF-8 on every platform (`_wfopen` on Windows).
- **Takes:** a take ends where the song position jumps (a loop wrap) and the next starts there, so loop recording gives a take per pass, all placed at the same start (take lanes and comping are the app's); `punch_in_steps`/`punch_out_steps` record only that range, sample-accurately.
- **Latency:** recorded frame n is placed at the song frame heard n frames of round-trip latency earlier (the writer keeps the chunk positions), so a take lands where it was played; `start_frame`/`start_steps` may be before 0 (trim that much). The latency is an override (`caw_engine_set_input_latency`), else the last good ping, else the device's estimate (both sides' buffers, plus the input bridge's starting fill, shown as `duplex_buffer_frames` (the name kept for the app), plus the output's block FIFO's mean hold; **[Assumption]** the estimate is close but not exact, since the fill drifts while running, so the ping is the reliable value). The ping (`caw_latency_ping_start`) plays a 4,096-sample noise burst at −12 dBFS after the limiter and cross-correlates it (by FFT, outside the control lock) with a second of input; the peak's lag is the round trip, accepted when it stands 8 times above the mean (tested with a simulated 733-frame loopback).
- **Device latency (API 23):** `caw_audio_latency` reports how late the running device is, per side, in engine frames: `"output"` from a sample leaving the engine to the speaker, `"input"` from the microphone to the engine reading it (the input bridge's starting fill included), each with its `"source"`, `"device_id"` and `"kind"`. On AAudio the figure is read from `AAudioStream_getTimestamp` (the time a frame was played or recorded) against `AAudioStream_getFramesWritten`/`getFramesRead`, the reckoning of Oboe's `calculateLatencyMillis`, under the device's reroute lock so a reroute cannot swap the stream mid-read (**[Fact, miniaudio 0.11.25 source, 2026-11-11]** `ma_job_process__device__aaudio_reroute` holds `aaudio.rerouteLock` while it closes and reopens the streams); `"device_id"` is `AAudioStream_getDeviceId`, the routed device's `AudioDeviceInfo.getId()`. Before the first timestamp it is what the output queues (`"buffers"`): on AAudio the stream's buffer size (`AAudioStream_getBufferSizeInFrames`), at the fixed 1024-frame size too, where miniaudio's periods are the stream's whole capacity (#241: the capacity overstated the delay; `queuedOutputFrames` in `src/device_period.h`, `the_output_latency_counts_the_frames_queued_not_the_buffer_capacity`); on WASAPI, ALSA and PulseAudio every queued period. The status's `periods` and the recording latency estimate count the same queue. `"kind"` (the app's `AudioDeviceKind` names) is read on WASAPI from the endpoint's property store, cached per endpoint id: the bus that enumerated it (`DEVPKEY_Device_EnumeratorName`; **[Assumption]** `BTHENUM` is classic Bluetooth, `BTHLE…` Bluetooth LE, `USB` USB audio, the bus names Windows uses in hardware ids, not read on a primary page), else its `PKEY_AudioEndpoint_FormFactor` (**[Fact, Microsoft Learn, read 2026-11-12]** VT_UI4 holding an `EndpointFormFactor`: RemoteNetworkDevice 0, Speakers, LineLevel, Headphones, Microphone, Headset, Handset, UnknownDigitalPassthrough, SPDIF, DigitalAudioDisplayDevice (HDMI or DisplayPort), UnknownFormFactor); ole32 is loaded at run time. Elsewhere `"unknown"` (Android's kind comes from `AudioManager` in the app). The Windows code is compiled by MinGW here and not yet run on Windows. **[Assumption]** whether a Bluetooth link's own delay is inside AAudio's timestamp is not documented (no primary source found, 2026-11-11), so the app adds a per-device offset on top only where its preset expects more than the platform reports. Without a device (`audio_none.cpp`) both sides read `"none"` (`latency_without_a_device_reports_none`).
- **Fixed engine blocks at the device (the owner's idea, 2026-11-12):** the output device (always playback only) renders the engine in whole 64-frame blocks (`kBlock`) whatever size its callback is, through `BlockFifo` (`engine/src/block_fifo.h`) inside `DeviceRender` (`engine/src/device_render.h`, which the callback and the tests share; no allocation, no lock). The callback first plays what the last one held, renders whole blocks straight into its output, and renders one more block for a tail, holding the rest (at most 63 frames) for the next callback. A callback that is whole blocks passes straight through and adds nothing; otherwise it holds up to 63 frames (1.3 ms at 48 kHz), on average (64 − gcd(period, 64)) / 2 (`DeviceRender::meanHold`; 31 or 32 at most periods), which `caw_audio_latency`'s output adds (`the_device_fifo_reports_its_mean_hold_for_alignment`). A period that is not whole blocks and is under 4 blocks (256 frames; a phone's 96- or 240-frame low-latency burst) bypasses the FIFO and calls `Engine::process` with the callback's frames: through the FIFO a 96-frame callback would render 128 and 64 frames by turns, 1.33 times its budget at the peaks; from 4 blocks the most is 1.25 times (257 frames), 1.14 at 441. The FIFO is new at every open and reopen (a sample-rate or route change reopens) and is never flushed while running: a Play, Stop or seek keeps the frames it holds, since the engine has already rendered them and moved on (dropping them jumped the waveform: a click), and a Stop's fade covers the Stop. **[Fact, miniaudio 0.11.25 source, `ma_device_init` and `ma_device__on_data`, read 2026-11-12]** miniaudio already calls back with a fixed size by default (`noFixedSizedCallback` off: an intermediary buffer of `periodSizeInFrames`, or of the backend's own period when that is 0), so the device's own sizes (Bluetooth's 441 or 1920) do not reach the callback; the FIFO makes the engine's block the same on every route on top of that. Asking miniaudio for a whole-block period instead would not help where the backend's burst is not whole blocks: the intermediary buffer would then bridge the two sizes with the same uneven work. That intermediary buffer's own hold-back is not in the latency figure yet. The input does not change the path (switching while running would drop or repeat the held frames): `DeviceRender::render` pulls the input bridge's next frames for each block it renders, so monitoring and recording stay in step with the frames rendered, not the frames played. The bridge's starting fill covers the output callback as miniaudio calls it (the larger of the backend's period and its fixed callback size, `intermediaryBufferCap`: on Android 11 and older AAudio ignores the size asked for, so a 96-frame burst can sit under a 512-frame callback) plus the FIFO's most (a callback through it pulls up to 63 frames more than it plays); that fill is queued input, a real delay, so `caw_audio_latency`'s `"input"` includes it. The output side adds the FIFO's mean hold, and the recording estimate (`setDeviceLatency`) is the two sides' buffers, the fill and the mean hold: each delay counted once. Whether the FIFO is used goes by the period only (`DeviceRender::suits`, decided at open). Tests: `sample_timing_test.cpp` (bit-identical to fixed blocks for random and Bluetooth-like sizes, the most held, a Stop or re-Play mid-note playing exactly the engine's render), `odd_callback_sizes_through_the_device_fifo_shed_nothing_under_the_threshold` and `the_block_fifo_never_allocates_on_the_audio_thread`.
- **Long runs and callback sizes (2026-11-12, the owner's Bluetooth report):** `caw_bench SECONDS 600` plays a suite scenario for many minutes in device-like callbacks (fast-forwarded) and prints each minute's mean, p99 and worst time per millisecond of audio, with no app calls (as backgrounded) or with `--app` (the app's read-only polls at their rates in audio time on another thread); `caw_bench SECONDS 601` renders every track tapped in 64-frame and in given (`--frames`, `--random`) callbacks at a `--rate` and reports where they differ, for a suite scenario or a project file (`--only FILE.json`, the engine's project JSON). Measured on Linux x64 (Release, 2026-11-12): song8 15 minutes flat at about 101–112 µs per ms of audio with and without the polls, First loop 30 minutes at random 1–2048-frame callbacks flat at about 21–24; per-track output bit-identical to 64-frame callbacks for song8 (441 and 1023 frames, 5 min; random at 44.1 kHz, 4 min), 32 tracks with heavy effects (1920, 3 min), First loop (960, 5 min) and the default project eight times over (random, 4 min), except one difference the default project showed: its Track 3's ducker (Tempo, waking a frame after a beat) ducked at 441-frame callbacks and not at 64, fixed above. Tests: `every_track_plays_the_same_bits_for_odd_callback_sizes_over_a_long_render`, `the_engine_does_the_same_work_after_minutes_of_play_with_no_app_calls`, `the_ducker_in_tempo_mode_wakes_the_same_whatever_the_block_size`.
- **Tracks against each other with the input on (#241, 2026-11-18):** `device_drift_test.cpp` plays four minutes of a song with a MIDI track (the test signal's impulse on each beat), drums, a 48 kHz and a 44.1 kHz clip and a 2x Signalsmith clip through `DeviceRender::openVarying` at AAudio-like callback sizes (37 to 1920 frames, jittered), with the input bridge open and fed by a capture 300 ppm fast, an audio track monitoring it, and the device's load reported. Every song track plays exactly the samples it plays in 64-frame blocks with no input (also with the mix pool on 3 workers); each beat lands on its frame (the impulse and the 48 kHz clip to the sample, the 44.1 kHz clip within 2 frames, the stretched clip within its 70 to 180-frame pre-echo, the same every loop); the monitored input's lag stays within 64 frames after 30 s with no underrun or skip; the input opened and closed six times mid-play changes nothing the song tracks play; and layers shed under load come back on their beats to the sample. So the engine's song clock counts only frames rendered for the output, at the engine's rate, the one rate every stream is asked for (`caw_audio_status` `engine_sample_rate`; `sample_rate` is the stream's own, different only where miniaudio resamples between them; AAudio resampling below the stream, such as to a Bluetooth codec's 44.1 kHz, is one clock for the whole output). What this does not cover, a device does: the stream's own underruns and frames the speaker took without a callback. **`caw_audio_status` `"clock"`** says what the output has done since it opened: `seconds`, `callbacks`, `frames` (their sum: frames / seconds is the rate the device really takes them at), `rendered_frames` (more by at most the FIFO's 63), `min_frames`, `max_frames`, `late_callbacks` (over their own budget), `xruns` (AAudio's `AAudioStream_getXRunCount`, NDK API 26; −1 elsewhere) and the layers `shed`, `restored` and `muted_now`; the input adds `pushed_frames` and `seconds` (its real rate, against the output's), `fill_frames` (the bridge's fill: the input's delay), `skipped_frames` and its own `xruns`. Relaxed counters written on each callback's thread only, reset at each open. Tests: `every_track_stays_on_its_beats_for_minutes_through_the_device_path_with_the_input_on`, `opening_and_closing_the_input_mid_play_keeps_every_track_in_time`, `a_layer_shed_under_load_comes_back_on_its_beats`.
- **Audio threads (API 24 on its branch, in the library from 28; speed phase 5d, 2026-11-12):** `caw_audio_threads` returns JSON: the core the callback ran on (`"callback_cpu"`, sampled every 32 callbacks with `sched_getcpu` or `GetCurrentProcessorNumber`), its `"core_kind"` (`big`, `little`, `other` when every core is one kind, `unknown`), that core's clock now and its top (`"clock_khz"`, `"max_khz"`: sysfs `cpufreq/scaling_cur_freq` and `cpuinfo_max_freq` on Linux and Android, `CallNtPowerInformation` on Windows, read on the calling thread, never the audio thread; −1 where unreadable), `"hint"` (an Android performance hint session is open), `"mmcss"`, what the platform granted the callback and the workers (`"callback_tuning"`, `"worker_tuning"`: `priority`, `realtime`, `fast_cores`, `mmcss`, `no_power_throttling`), `"workers"`, `"active_workers"`, `"wait_share"`, the load stabilizer's `"stabilizer"` (`off`, `idle`, `on`), `"stabilizer_share"` and `"stabilizer_work"` (Android, below) and the machine's `"cores"`, `"fast_cores"`, `"hybrid"` and `"core_source"`. The app reads it through `Engine.audioThreads()` in `app/lib/engine/engine.dart` (no UI yet; telemetry samples it every 5 s while playing for H2 `perf.callbackCoreType` and `perf.schedHint`, [app](app.md)). The platform parts are behind `src/thread_tuning.h`, loaded at run time with a silent fallback:
  - **Core kinds:** one number per core (Linux and Android `cpu_capacity`, else `cpuinfo_max_freq`; Windows the CPU sets' `EfficiencyClass`); cores within 15% of the slowest are little and the rest big when they differ more, else all `other` (`classifyCores`).
  - **Android:** workers at nice −19 (`THREAD_PRIORITY_URGENT_AUDIO`; **[Fact, developer.android.com `android.os.Process`, read 2026-11-12]** −19, "Applications should not normally change to this priority", so the request falls back to −16, −10, −8, −4, −2) when the callback is real-time (AAudio's low-latency callback; **[Assumption]** it is `SCHED_FIFO` there: the rule reads its policy at run time), else the callback's own nice; kept to the big cores on a hybrid phone (a mask, never one core; the callback is never pinned). **The performance hint** (ADPF `APerformanceHint`) is asked for the callback and the workers in every stream mode, real-time or not (shared, Bluetooth, low-latency, the playing buffer), with the callback period as its target: **[Fact, NDK r30 `android/performance_hint.h`, read 2026-11-17]** the threads "must be part of this process' thread group", nothing about their policy; **[Fact, AOSP `HintManagerService.java` main, read 2026-11-17]** the service checks only that each thread is the app's (`checkTidValid`), and for a `SCHED_OTHER` thread adds `SCHED_RESET_ON_FORK`, so it expects time-sharing threads, and it refuses every session when the power HAL has none (`isHintSessionSupported`: a preferred rate of −1); **[Fact, Oboe main `AudioStreamAAudio.cpp`, read 2026-11-17]** Oboe opens one for any AAudio callback thread when asked (`beginPerformanceHintInCallback`, no mode check). A refused session is asked for again after 1 s, doubling to 30 s (`hintRetryNs`; before #234 it waited for the callback's thread to change, which on one stream never happens), and for the callback alone if the group was refused. Each callback's own duration goes to the system, every one in order (at most the newest 64 between two of the hint thread's turns; before #234 the longest since the last report). `caw_audio_threads` says why one is or isn't open (`"hint_state"`: `none` no API, `waiting`, `on`, `failed`, `unsupported` the system says it has none: Android 16's `APerformanceHint_isFeatureSupported`, else that preferred rate) and the callback's `"callback_policy"` and `"callback_nice"`; the Benchmark's live rows keep that JSON (`audio_threads`). **[Fact, NDK r30]** `APerformanceHint_getManager`, `createSession`, `getPreferredUpdateRateNanos` (deprecated in API 36), `updateTargetWorkDuration`, `reportActualWorkDuration` and `closeSession` are API 33, `setThreads` API 34, `isFeatureSupported` API 36; "methods of APerformanceHintSession_* are not thread safe". **[Fact, developer.android.com/ndk/reference/group/a-performance-hint, read 2026-11-12]** the reference says nothing on calling `reportActualWorkDuration` from a real-time thread, so the callback only stores its durations in atomics and the hint's own thread makes every system call, at the preferred rate clamped to 10–100 ms. Looked up with `dlopen("libandroid.so")`, so Android 10–12 run without it. **[Assumption]** what the system does with the hint (clocks, core placement) is the power HAL's. **[Fact, the owner's S22+ Benchmark, 2026-11-19]** `hint_state` is `unsupported` there (the power HAL has no sessions) and the callback's big core ran at 634 MHz of 2.5 GHz. **The load stabilizer** (`src/load_stabilizer.h`, `stabilize` in `platform/audio_miniaudio.cpp`): where no hint session is open, with the low-latency profile asked (not Battery; whatever AAudio granted, since the S22+ grants no low-latency mode at its 960–1024-frame buffers), while something sounds and the app is in front (`caw_audio_set_background` also stops it), the callback spins after its work and its timing until a share of its period from its ideal start, Oboe's `StabilizedCallback` arithmetic reimplemented, and only while its own work's smoothed share of the period says the clock matters (on above 0.16, off below 0.04, or after an overrun; `LoadStabilizer::wanted`); its epoch follows clock drift and stalls, and the callback that starts one spins nothing; Android only. The share (1.1.1, API 45; `LoadStabilizer::spinShare`) is only as much as the clock needs: 0.8 (which holds the clock) while the callbacks' recent peak work (up at once, down over 3 s) is at or over a target share of the period, and proportionally less below it (never under 0.48), so a clock a burst raised settles where the slowest callbacks take about the target; the target is 0.5, 0.6 with the platform's thermal status warm and 0.7 hot, and at critical it does not spin (`caw_audio_set_thermal(engine, CAW_PRESSURE_*)`, which the app calls with `AppState.pressure` on every change). Why and what it costs: [performance](performance.md#live-callback-clock-and-the-benchmark).
  - **Windows:** miniaudio's WASAPI thread joins MMCSS "Pro Audio" (`cfg.wasapi.usage`; **[Fact, miniaudio 0.11.25 source, 2026-11-12]** `ma_device_start__wasapi_nolock` calls `AvSetMmThreadCharacteristicsA` on the audio thread only for a usage other than the default, and C.A.W had left the default), and the workers join it too when it did (else `THREAD_PRIORITY_HIGHEST`, miniaudio's own thread priority); both opt out of power throttling (`SetThreadInformation`, `ThreadPowerThrottling` with `ControlMask` execution speed and `StateMask` 0: **[Fact, Microsoft Learn SetThreadInformation, read 2026-11-12]** HighQoS, the documented example); on a hybrid CPU the workers keep to the highest `EfficiencyClass` (`SetThreadSelectedCpuSets`; **[Fact, Microsoft Learn SYSTEM_CPU_SET_INFORMATION, read 2026-11-12]** "higher numerical values … are faster but less power-efficient", Windows 10+). `AvSetMmThreadCharacteristicsW` is avrt.dll (Windows Vista+, task names under `HKLM\…\Multimedia\SystemProfile\Tasks`). Compiled by MinGW here; not yet run on Windows.
  - **Linux:** `SCHED_FIFO` one step below the callback, only when the callback itself is real-time (miniaudio's default thread on Linux is not: **[Fact, miniaudio 0.11.25 source]** `ma_thread_priority_highest` keeps the current policy), else no better than the callback's nice; refused requests change nothing. **[Proposal]** rtkit over D-Bus for an unprivileged real-time callback and workers (libdbus's licence is not clearly permissive: "Each platform's low-latency path" above; not added).
  - **macOS, iOS:** nothing yet (`thread_tuning_none.cpp`).
  - **Rule:** a worker's priority is never above the callback's: a spinning worker above the callback could take its core and starve it.
- **Recovery:** after a crash, `caw_record_recover(dir)` (refused while a recording in this process writes into `dir`; a `.wav.part` whose 44-byte header is not the recorder's own is left alone) fixes each `.wav.part`'s RIFF sizes to the whole frames written (the header is also brought up to date about every second while recording), renames it and lists it with its sidecar's placement and `"recovered": true`.


### Performance

[Fact, this container's 2.3 GHz Xeon, Release, 2026-11-11, 256-frame callbacks at 48 kHz, one core]: a clip at its file's speed costs about 0.09% of a core, varispeed 0.13%, WSOLA 0.27% (0.32% with pitch); a sampler voice with its filter on 0.15%. A phone core is slower ([performance](performance.md)); WSOLA's search is the part to watch.

[Fact, this container's Xeon, Release, 2026-11-14, the whole engine playing one clip in 256-frame callbacks at 48 kHz, `signalsmith_prepares_once_and_a_voice_costs_this_much`]: plain playback 0.24–0.43% of a core, WSOLA at ratio 1.1 0.42–0.59%, Signalsmith at ratio 1.1 1.42% (1.54–1.57% with 3 semitones of pitch): a Signalsmith voice costs about 1.1% of a core, five times plain playback and WSOLA's two to three times. Its split computation keeps a 64-frame block's worst under about 110 µs (423 µs unsplit, the cost of a whole spectrum at once; a standalone probe of the library). A stretcher's preparation takes 4.5 ms on the control thread. A track's eight Signalsmith voices at most (the cap) would take about 9% of this core; a phone core is slower (the S22+'s figure is to measure).

## The `caw` CLI

```
caw render PROJECT.json -o OUT.wav [--loop | --seconds S | --oneshot LAYER [--note N --velocity V --note-seconds S] | --wavetable TARGET --param P --frames N]
           [--rate 44100|48000] [--float]
caw midi import IN.mid -o OUT.caw | caw midi export PATTERN.caw -o OUT.mid [--clip]
caw test-component ID|LIBRARY [--id ID --trust T] [--signal TYPE ...] [--param NAME=VALUE ...] [--sweep P] [--compare X] [--bypass] [--report json|text] [--strict]
caw sample-pack [INSTRUMENT] --out DIR [--request FILE.json] [--library PATH --trust T] [--effect ID ...] [--notes ...] [--vel ...] [--step NAME=V1,V2 ...] [...]
caw scan FILE [--json] [--plugin] [--id ID] [--legacy-grace]
```

- `caw scan` runs the [pre-load scan](#pre-load-scan) as the loader does: the verdict, SHA-256, declarations and one line per finding, or `--json` (the C API's JSON). `--plugin` weighs it as a CLAP/VST3 plug-in, `--id` checks a `caw-component.json` beside the file, `--legacy-grace` applies the grace. It exits 2 when blocked, 1 when the file can't be read, 0 otherwise.

- It prints frames, length, peak and RMS, and flags `SILENT` or `CLIPPING`, so an agent can check its work without listening.

## Pre-load scan

Before a native library is opened (opening already runs its constructors), the engine looks at its bytes (`engine/src/scan/`, 1.0.7). Every load goes through it (`ModuleRegistry::loadSharedLibrary`: the app's load and reload, the CLI's `test-component` and `sample-pack`, the plug-in benchmark), on the calling control thread, never the audio thread. Built-ins are not files and are never scanned. `caw scan` and `caw_engine_scan_component` run the same scan without loading anything.

- **Reader:** the engine's own, no dependency. ELF64 little-endian (any machine; AArch64 and x86-64 get the system-call check) and PE32+ (x86-64, Arm64). Every byte is hostile: the file is read up to 256 MiB, and the reader caps sections (4,096; PE 96), program headers (512), dynamic entries (8,192), dynamic symbols (2^20), relocations (2^22), import libraries (1,024) and imports per library (65,536), TLS callbacks (1,024), names (1,024 bytes) and the table entries walked in all (2^26); every offset and size is checked against the file without overflow. Anything inconsistent or past a limit is a **malformed** finding, never undefined behaviour; a file that is not ELF64 LE or PE32+ (ELF32, PE32, Mach-O, anything else) is **unsupported**. Layouts the loaders read differently are malformed too: an ELF dynamic table whose address (`p_vaddr`, where glibc and bionic read it) and file offset disagree, overlapping or unsorted loadable segments; a PE whose FileAlignment or SectionAlignment Windows would refuse, sections out of order, overlapping or unaligned, or a raw pointer off a 512-byte boundary (Windows rounds it down). Imports come from what the loader itself uses: ELF's dynamic table (its symbol count from the section headers, `DT_HASH`, `DT_GNU_HASH` and the relocations' highest index, Android's packed `DT_ANDROID_REL`/`RELA` (APS2) relocations included, decoded as bionic's `packed_reloc_iterator` reads them, from memory of its source: **[Assumption]** not yet checked on an NDK-built file), PE's import and delay-import directories (an import by ordinal counts through its DLL).
- **Findings** (`scan.h`): each has a capability id (`internet`, `read_files`, `write_files`, `start_programs`, `load_code`, `make_code`, `android_system`, `background_threads`, `encryption`, `input_spying`, `packed`), a kind (`import`, `library`, `export`, `syscall`, `section`, `entropy`, `string`, `load_time`, `malformed`, `unsupported`, `declarations`, `dependency`), evidence (a symbol, `dll!name`, a library, an offset or a string, at most 160 printable ASCII characters), a strength (`certain`: the binary has it; `hint`: the bytes suggest it) and its own severity (`notice` for files and threads, `warning` for the rest), which is how a CLAP/VST3 plug-in is weighed. What it looks for:
  - Imports by name (`analyze.cpp`, `kImports`): networking (`socket`, `connect`, `getaddrinfo`, `send`/`recv`, curl, WinINet, WinHTTP, Winsock, `URLDownloadToFile`), starting programs and reaching into other processes (`exec*`, `fork`, `system`, `popen`, `posix_spawn`, `ptrace`, `process_vm_*`, `CreateProcess*`, `ShellExecute*`, `WinExec`, `WriteProcessMemory`, `CreateRemoteThread`, `VirtualAllocEx`), loading code (`dlopen`, `dlsym`, `android_dlopen_ext`, `LoadLibrary*`, `GetProcAddress`), making code (`pkey_mprotect`; `mprotect`, `memfd_create`, `VirtualProtect`, `VirtualAlloc` as hints, since runtimes use them), Android (`JNI_CreateJavaVM`, `AAssetManager_fromJava`, sensors, camera, AAudio, OpenSL ES, and an exported `JNI_OnLoad`, `JNI_OnUnload` or `Java_*`), threads (`pthread_create`, `thrd_create`, `CreateThread`, `_beginthread*`, libstdc++'s `std::thread`), keyboard, clipboard and the registry (`SetWindowsHookEx`, `GetAsyncKeyState`, `*Clipboard*`, `XGrabKeyboard`, `Reg*`), encryption (OpenSSL, mbed TLS, libgcrypt and libsodium families, CryptoAPI, CNG), reading files (`fopen`, `opendir`, `CreateFile`, `ReadFile`, `FindFirstFile`, file streams and `std::filesystem` reads) and writing files (`unlink`, `rename`, `mkdir`, `truncate`, `WriteFile`, `DeleteFile`, `MoveFile`, `std::filesystem` writes). A PE name is also tried without its A/W suffix; members of `std::filesystem::path` (text only) are not file access. Needed libraries: libcurl, libssl, mbed TLS (internet); libcrypto, libsodium, libgcrypt, ncrypt.dll (encryption); ws2_32, wsock32, winhttp, wininet, urlmon, dnsapi (internet); libnativehelper, libcamera2ndk, libaaudio, libOpenSLES, libjnigraphics (Android).
  - **Dependencies:** a component may need only system libraries: ELF `libc`, `libm`, `libdl`, `libpthread`, `librt`, `libstdc++`, `libgcc_s`, `libc++_shared`, `libc++`, `libc++abi`, `libatomic`, `libmvec`, the dynamic linkers, `liblog`, `libandroid`, `libaaudio`, `libOpenSLES`, `libmediandk`, `libnativewindow`, `libz`; PE Windows system DLLs (kernel32, ntdll, user32, advapi32, ws2_32, …), the API sets (`api-ms-win-*`, `ext-ms-win-*`) and the MSVC/UCRT runtime (`ucrtbase`, `vcruntime140*`, `msvcp140*`, `concrt140`, `vcomp140`; not MinGW's `libstdc++-6.dll`, `libgcc_s_seh-1.dll` or `libwinpthread-1.dll`). Any other needed library, and any `DT_RPATH`/`DT_RUNPATH`, is a `dependency` finding: the system loader would load code the scan never saw (for example `$ORIGIN` and a `libpayload.so` beside it). It blocks a component outright, grace or not; a plug-in only warns. Windows loads a component with `LOAD_LIBRARY_SEARCH_DEFAULT_DIRS` (the application folder and System32, never the current folder or PATH). Imports by ordinal: from ws2_32, wsock32, wininet, winhttp, urlmon or dnsapi they are internet (certain); from any other DLL a hint warning (the function is hidden). ntdll's `Nt*`/`Zw*` calls count too (`NtCreateFile` and friends as files, `NtDeviceIoControlFile` as internet, `NtCreateThreadEx` as threads), as do `clone` (threads) and `_r_debug` (load code). Windows threads: `CreateThread`, the UCRT's `_beginthread(ex)`, the STL's `_Thrd_create`/`_Thrd_start` and the system thread pool (`CreateThreadpoolWork`, `SubmitThreadpoolWork`, `TrySubmitThreadpoolCallback`, `CreateThreadpoolTimer`, `QueueUserWorkItem`, `CreateTimerQueueTimer`).
  - **The C++ runtime is allowed:** `__cxa_*`, `operator new`, `pthread_mutex_*`/`once`/`key`/`cond`, locale and stdio calls are not findings. `open`, `open64`, `openat` and `__open_2` (`std::random_device`'s `/dev/urandom` in a statically linked runtime) are hints shown as notices; `fopen`, file streams and `std::filesystem` are certain, and a file opened (`fopen`, a stream) together with a write import (`fwrite`, `fputs`, `fprintf`, `std::ostream`) is also `write_files`, since the open's mode can't be seen. `dl_iterate_phdr` (the unwinder walks the libraries on every exception) and `memfd_create` (every NDK static build imports it; running from the memfd still needs `mmap`/`mprotect` or `dlopen`, which are counted) are hints. **`syscall()`** reaches any system call by number, so a static C++ runtime imports it and a component could hide `connect` or `execve` behind it. On **AArch64** (the shipped Android ABI) the scan reads every call site (`syscall_sites.cpp`): it finds `syscall`'s PLT stub through its GOT relocation, every `bl`/tail `b` to the stub, and the constant moved into `w0`/`x0` just before (within 8 instructions, stopping at a label or branch). If every call passes a number only the C++ runtimes use — futex (98), gettid (178) or rt_sigprocmask (135) — the import is a notice; any other number, a non-constant argument, a call it can't read, `syscall`'s address taken (a non-`JUMP_SLOT` relocation), or its GOT slot reached without the stub makes it certain (fails closed). This was checked 2026-11-18 by building the starters and a `std::call_once`/`std::mutex`/function-local-static component with NDK r30 (`-static-libstdc++`, `android26`) and host GCC 13, both AArch64: all notice. **x86-64 is scanned strictly** — its variable-length encoding makes a precise call-site reader far larger, and no x86-64 component ships (Android is arm64-only, Windows uses the MSVC runtime, which has no `syscall` import), so an x86-64 component that calls the imported `syscall` is certain whatever the number. An imported `syscall` that is never called is a notice. The NDK's static libc++abi does import `syscall` (gettid for its guards), so this analysis, not a runtime switch, is what lets the app's on-device AArch64 builds pass.
